## Crime

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=53 "Edit section: Crime")\]

Accurate statistics on crime are not available for Lagos. Residents may not report crimes to the police due to lack of access, distrust, fear of retaliation, or the belief that the police will simply be unable to help. The police, in turn, suffer from chronic underfunding, poor equipment, inadequate training, and corruption, limiting their ability to investigate crimes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-264)

The [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations "Council on Foreign Relations")' Nigeria Security Tracker[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-:13) tracked [homicides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homicide "Homicide") ( [murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder "Murder"), [manslaughter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manslaughter "Manslaughter"), etc.) by criminals, religious zealots (such as Boko Haram) and police attacks in Nigeria for each state from 2012 to 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-266)

Strikingly, according to the NST, Nigeria's two largest cities, Lagos and [Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_(city) "Kano (city)"), are significantly safer than rural areas when it comes to homicides. For Lagos State, the NST shows 135 such violent deaths in the past 24 months (as of July 2023), or 5.6 annually for every million inhabitants (for comparison, the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom "United Kingdom")'s crime statistics in 2022 counted 10 homicides per million inhabitants[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-267) or [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey "Turkey")'s 24[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-268)). For Nigeria as a whole, this figure is 93, with 62% of these cases attributable to [police violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality "Police brutality") (mainly [vigilante justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigilantism "Vigilantism")). [Boko Haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boko_Haram "Boko Haram") plays a negligible role (as of 2023) in Lagos.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-1)[\[263\]](#refnote-:13)

## Parks and recreation

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=54 "Edit section: Parks and recreation")\]

### Parks and gardens

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=55 "Edit section: Parks and gardens")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Lagos-Freedom-Park-Pond-Aug-2026-IMG_6724.jpg/250px-Lagos-Freedom-Park-Pond-Aug-2026-IMG_6724.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lagos-Freedom-Park-Pond-Aug-2026-IMG_6724.jpg) Freedom Park (2026)

_[Freedom Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Park_(Lagos) "Freedom Park (Lagos)")_ is a memorial and recreational park in the middle of Lagos city centre on Lagos Island, Nigeria, which used to be Her Majesty's Broad Street Prison. It was designed by architect Theo Lawson. The park was built to preserve the history and cultural heritage of Nigerians. The monuments in the park commemorate the colonial heritage of Lagos and the history of Her Majesty's Broad Street Prison. It was erected to mark the 50th Independence Anniversary celebrations in October 2010. The park is a national memorial, a historical landmark, a cultural site and an arts and recreation centre. Now a tranquil place for individuals, visitors and collective contemplation, the park is open to the public daily. Today, Freedom Park has become a gathering place for various social events and recreational entertainment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-269)

_[Muri Okunola Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muri_Okunola_Park "Muri Okunola Park")_ on [Victoria Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Island,_Lagos "Victoria Island, Lagos") is adorned with three works of art: sculpture _MA´MI_ by Abolore Sobayo (2017), _Undressed Identity_ by Dudu Emmanuel (2018) and _"The King's Court"_ by artist collective _Inscribe_ (2026). It is often used by photographers and content creators as a backdrop.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-270)

_[Ndubuisi Kanu Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ndubuisi_Kanu_Park "Ndubuisi Kanu Park")_ and _[Johnson Jakande Tinubu (JJT) Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Jakande_Tinubu_Park "Johnson Jakande Tinubu Park")_ are public green spaces in the district of [Ikeja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikeja "Ikeja"), situated in the immediate vicinity of the [Lagos State House of Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_State_House_of_Assembly "Lagos State House of Assembly"), the official residence of the State Governor and other government buildings (Ikeja is the administrative centre of [Lagos State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_State "Lagos State")). They are separated from one another by Mobolaji Johnson Avenue. – Johnson Jakande Tinubu Park is characterised by a sculpture in which the heads of the three (former) governors after whom the park is named merge into one another.

The _Jhalobia Recreation Park and Gardens_ is a botanical garden run by Veronica Adepoju and her two daughters – three qualified landscape designers who showcase the full range of their artistry on just under a hectare of land. Situated in the immediate vicinity of the airport, the site is very photogenic and is therefore regularly booked for weddings and other celebrations, but it is also ideal for tourists who have arrived too early for their return flight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-271)[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-272)

_[MKO Abiola Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MKO_Abiola_Park "MKO Abiola Park")_ and the adjacent _Peace Park_ are public green spaces in the Ketu district with sculptures. The former is named after [Moshood Abiola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moshood_Abiola "Moshood Abiola"), the presumed winner of the 1993 elections, who died as a political prisoner under the dictator [Abacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sani_Abacha "Sani Abacha"); there is a statue of him in the park.

### Nature parks

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=56 "Edit section: Nature parks")\]

The _Lekki Conservation Centre_ is located in Lekki Peninsula II. It consists of a fenced-off strip of vegetation that attempts to replicate rainforest, mangroves and savannah. There are monkeys, numerous birds, snakes and crocodiles. There is a small museum with stuffed animals. On Sundays however, it is used for lengthy religious service. According to a tourism website, the LCC is the second-most popular site[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-273) in Lagos to see (after the Nike Gallery, see [chapter "Art"](#Art)).

_[LUFASI Nature Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LUFASI_Park "LUFASI Park")_ is the Lekki Urban Forestry and Animal Shelter Initiative in Sangotedo.[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-274) It preserves nature and protects wildlife and endangered species. Nollywood film crews often use this park as a set.

## Transportation

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=57 "Edit section: Transportation")\]

Main article: [Transport in Lagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Lagos "Transport in Lagos")

Lagos has one of the largest and most extensive road networks in West Africa.[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-275)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BRT-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-BRT) It also has suburban trains and some ferry services. Highways are usually [congested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_congestion "Traffic congestion") in peak hours owing to the geography of the city and to its explosive population growth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-africareport-1)[\[83\]](#refnote-africareport)[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-277) Lagos is also linked by many highways and bridges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-278)

### Expressways

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=58 "Edit section: Expressways")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Nigeria_Highways_01.jpg/250px-Nigeria_Highways_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nigeria_Highways_01.jpg) Expressway A1 at Lagos-Onipanu with bus lane

Since 2023, the renovation of existing motorways and the construction of new ones have been central to the government policy of President [Bola Tinubu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bola_Tinubu "Bola Tinubu"), who – through the floating of the [national currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_naira "Nigerian naira"), the abolition of [petrol subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidy "Subsidy") and the introduction of a general [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax "Income tax") – has been able to channel more funds into infrastructure than his predecessors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-279)[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-280) Motorways under the jurisdiction of the Nigerian federal government are in significantly better condition than the access roads (which are administered at federal and local levels).

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Lagos-Nigeria-Coastal-Highway-at-Victoria-Island-aug-2026-IMG_6851.jpg/250px-Lagos-Nigeria-Coastal-Highway-at-Victoria-Island-aug-2026-IMG_6851.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lagos-Nigeria-Coastal-Highway-at-Victoria-Island-aug-2026-IMG_6851.jpg) Beginning of the Coastal Highway in Lagos (2026)

Lagos is (as of 2026) connected to the surrounding area via several motorways (‘expressways’):

- the [F100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos%E2%80%93Badagry_Expressway "Lagos–Badagry Expressway"); heading west towards [Badagry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badagry "Badagry") and [Porto-Novo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto-Novo "Porto-Novo") in the Republic of [Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin "Benin") – its renovation was largely completed in 2026,[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-281)[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-282)
- the [A5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A5_highway_(Nigeria) "A5 highway (Nigeria)"); heading north to [Abeokuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abeokuta "Abeokuta") and on to [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan "Ibadan") – Nigeria’s busiest expressway (over 250,000 PCU daily), currently undergoing renovation (as of September 2026); contractor Craneburg reported in 2025 that 50 per cent of the renovation work had been completed,[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-283)
- the [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_highway_(Nigeria) "A1 highway (Nigeria)"); heading north-east directly to [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan "Ibadan") – another key transport artery carrying just under 250,000 PCU daily between Nigeria’s largest and third-largest cities; the Nigerian federal government approved its renovation in 2026;[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-284)
- the [Lekki-Epe Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lekki%E2%80%93Epe_Expressway "Lekki–Epe Expressway") (no abbreviation) heading east-north-east; an intra-state motorway in Lagos State and one of the first Nigerian expressways on which a toll was levied;
- the Coastal Highway (no abbreviation as yet) running eastwards, initially from the city centre to the Free Trade Zone, the [Dangote Refinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangote_refinery "Dangote refinery") and the Lekki Deep-Sea Port (approximately 60 km), and upon completion as far as [Calabar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calabar "Calabar"); the President’s ‘pet project’, for which he bypassed parliamentary procedures to push through its construction and dispatched the first construction machinery to the planned section of the route just a few days after his swearing-in;[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-285)
- still under construction is the [Badagry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badagry "Badagry")– [Sokoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_(city) "Sokoto (city)") Expressway (another project of the Tinubu government), running northwards along the border with the Republic of Benin, which is intended to develop the structurally underdeveloped far west and will scrape the Lagos metropolitan area; the first section from Badagry to [Ilaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilaro "Ilaro") is (as of August 2026) complete, according to vloggers, and is being used by local traffic.[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-286)

Lagos's importance as a commercial centre and port and its strategic location have led to it being the end-point of three [Trans-African Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-African_Highway "Trans-African Highway") routes using Nigeria's national roads.[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-287) The [Trans–West African Coastal Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans%E2%80%93West_African_Coastal_Highway "Trans–West African Coastal Highway") leaves the city as the Badagry Expressway to [Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin "Benin") and beyond as far as [Dakar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakar "Dakar") and [Nouakchott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouakchott "Nouakchott"); the [Trans-Sahara Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Sahara_Highway "Trans-Sahara Highway") to [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers "Algiers"), which is close to completion, leaves the city as the Lagos-Ibadan Expressway.[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-288) – However, the term ‘ [Trans-African Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-African_Highway_network "Trans-African Highway network")’ (according to the Wikipedia entry) is ‘not in wide common usage outside of planning and development circles, and as of 2014[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit) one does not see them signposted as such or labelled on maps’. ‘Trans-African Highway’ therefore really only represents a planning proposal dating from 2007. Instead, the term ‘Trans-African _corridors_’ is therefore more commonly used. – Infrastructure planning in Africa that spans several states faces challenges given the political instability of some of the countries involved. Furthermore, due to existing disparities in prosperity, the [Economic Community of West African States, ECOWAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ECOWAS "ECOWAS"), is unable to fulfil the role in coordinating international transport projects that the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union"), for example, plays.

### Intercity railway line

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=59 "Edit section: Intercity railway line")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Lagos-Nigeria-Mobolaji-Johnson-railway-station.jpg/250px-Lagos-Nigeria-Mobolaji-Johnson-railway-station.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lagos-Nigeria-Mobolaji-Johnson-railway-station.jpg) Mobolaji Johnson, terminus station of the standard gauge railway line to [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan "Ibadan")

Since 2021, a railway line has linked Lagos to [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan "Ibadan") (via [Abeokuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abeokuta "Abeokuta")), a city situated 120 km further north-east, deep in the interior of the country (Ibadan is the country’s third-largest city after Lagos and [Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_(city) "Kano (city)")). The journey takes two and a half hours and costs 9,000 naira (6 euros) in first class. The locomotives, carriages and station buildings are new and modern.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Lagos-Ibadan_train_Experience-1)[\[30\]](#refnote-Lagos-Ibadan_train_Experience)[&#8202;](#ref-back-289-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-289) Six trains run daily on this line.[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-290)

The railway line, built to [standard gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard-gauge_railway "Standard-gauge railway") (1,436 mm), replaced a route dating from the colonial era and built to [‘Cape’ gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3_ft_6_in_gauge_railways "3 ft 6 in gauge railways") (1,056 mm between the rails). This allows for higher speeds. This new rail link is stimulating local economic growth.[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-291)

The Chinese state-owned enterprises [CCECC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Civil_Engineering_Construction_Corporation "China Civil Engineering Construction Corporation") and [CRRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CRRC "CRRC") played a decisive role in the construction and are evidence of the growing influence of the [People’s Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China") in [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa "Africa").

The rail link is managed by the [Nigerian Railway Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Railway_Corporation "Nigerian Railway Corporation"), which reports to the Nigerian federal government.

### Air traffic

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=60 "Edit section: Air traffic")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/MMIA_Intl_Terminal_2019.jpg/250px-MMIA_Intl_Terminal_2019.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MMIA_Intl_Terminal_2019.jpg) [Murtala Muhammed International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtala_Muhammed_International_Airport "Murtala Muhammed International Airport")

Lagos is served by [Murtala Muhammed International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtala_Muhammed_International_Airport "Murtala Muhammed International Airport"), one of the largest and [busiest airports in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Africa "List of the busiest airports in Africa"). The MMIA is Nigeria's premier international air gateway. The airport's history dates back to colonial times, around the time of the Second World War. The international airport terminal was built and commissioned over 40 years ago, in 1978. The terminal opened officially on 15 March 1979. The airport had been known simply as the Lagos International Airport. It was, however, renamed for the late Nigerian Head of State, General Murtala Muhammed, who died in 1976.[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-292)

The airport terminal has been renovated several times since the 1970s but its most radical makeover began in 2013, following the launch of the Federal government's multi-billion naira Remodelling/ Rehabilitation Programme for its airports nationwide. Under the re-modeling work there, by late in 2014, the MMA lounge area had been expanded to four times its previous size and new passenger handling conveyor systems were installed which can handle over 1,000 passengers per hour.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

A second airport, [Lekki-Epe International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lekki-Epe_International_Airport "Lekki-Epe International Airport") has been approved by the Federal Government in April 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-293)

### Local public transport

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=61 "Edit section: Local public transport")\]

The Lagos Metropolitan Area Transport Authority (LAMATA) is responsible for public transport.[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-294)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LAMATA_rail_service_expands_to_94_daily_trips-1)[\[34\]](#refnote-LAMATA_rail_service_expands_to_94_daily_trips)[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-295)[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-296)

Since 2021, using a bus or the light rail system is paid for with a public transport card without cash.[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-297)[&#8202;](#ref-back-298-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-298) This card can be used equally on BRT and LBSL buses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cowry_contactless_card_for_transit-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-Cowry_contactless_card_for_transit) One can purchase a public transportation card at any of the ticketing booths at the bus terminals scattered across Lagos State.[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-300)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Cowry_contactless_card_for_transit-1)[\[297\]](#refnote-Cowry_contactless_card_for_transit)

#### City buses

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=62 "Edit section: City buses")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/BRT_Bus.jpg/250px-BRT_Bus.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BRT_Bus.jpg) BRT bus in Lagos

There are two city bus companies in Lagos: BRT ( [Lagos Bus Rapid Transit System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Bus_Rapid_Transit_System "Lagos Bus Rapid Transit System")) and LBSL (Lagos Bus Services).[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-301)[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-302) The city buses are air-conditioned. (However, during the Covid epidemic, the AC had to be switched off at all times.)[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-303)[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-304)

BRT was inaugurated in 2008. BRT offers e-payment with bank cards.[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-305)\[ _[non-primary source needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:No_original_research#Primary,_secondary_and_tertiary_sources "Wikipedia:No original research")_\] On two arterial roads (Ikorodu Road and Funsho Williams Avenue), a dedicated bus lane has been established for BRT buses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-306) BRT uses diverse brands of buses, like Ashok Leyland and Yutong. Primero Transport Services (PTS) Ltd. is the sole operator of the BRT buses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-307)

Before the new big bus automated system, Lagos used to depend on the popular big bus called **"Molue"** which only very few can still be found plying the Lagos road especially within Oshodi - Iyana Ipaja to Sango route.

LBSL was inaugurated in 2019. LBSL uses Brazilian-built Marcopolo buses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-308)

The central hub for city buses and long-distance buses is the [Oshodi Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oshodi_Bus_Terminal "Oshodi Bus Terminal"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-309) which is visible from afar. It is the largest bus station in [West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Africa "West Africa") and commenced operation in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-310)

The Lagos Transportation Department, [LAMATA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Metropolitan_Area_Transport_Authority "Lagos Metropolitan Area Transport Authority"), introduced electric buses in Lagos in May 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-311)[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-312)

#### Metro rail

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=63 "Edit section: Metro rail")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Mile2station-best.jpg/250px-Mile2station-best.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mile2station-best.jpg) Station "Mile 2", current terminus station of the blue line (2026)

The rapid transit system, the [Lagos Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Light_Rail "Lagos Light Rail"), has been operational since February 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-313)[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-314) The " [Blue Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Line_(Lagos_Transit) "Blue Line (Lagos Transit)")" runs between Mile 2 and Marina (East-west axis). The '' [Red Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_(Lagos_Transit) "Red Line (Lagos Transit)")'' runs between [Agbado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifako-Ijaiye "Ifako-Ijaiye") and Oyingbo (North–south axis). The Lagos light rail system has been in operation since 2023 and, since 2024, has comprised two lines: the blue and the red lines. Both lines run above ground and, in the city centre, on stilts, i.e. a few metres above the ground.

Here, too, passengers use the ‘Cowry’ prepaid card, as is already the case on the city buses.

On 4 September 2026, the operator LAMATA announced on [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") that the blue line had carried 9 million passengers over the past 36 months across 70,000 scheduled train runs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-315)[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-316) A train runs every 10 minutes on the Blue Line. The network claims that 99.5 per cent of trains run on time.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In 2026, work will be completed on extending the Blue Line to Okokomaiko,[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-317) and the construction of a ‘Green Line’ is planned[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-318) (which is also to run partly on piles). The three lines are to be connected at Marina station (the [French Development Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Development_Agency "French Development Agency"), AFD, is involved in this project).

#### Shared cabs

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=64 "Edit section: Shared cabs")\]

A popular means of transportation are yellow minibuses called **"Danfo" or "Faragan"** as popularly called. The yellow buses, most of the VW T3 or LT type, characterize the appearance of the city. They run on fixed routes but without a timetable, according to the principle of shared cabs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-319)

#### Ferries

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=65 "Edit section: Ferries")\]

According to residents, getting to work by car in Lagos can take six times longer than by ferry. About two million passengers were recorded by the Ferry Authority per month in 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-320)

Five Cowries Terminal is the central terminal for ferry operations in Lagos.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-:7) The terminal is located on the lagoon between Lagos Island and Victoria Island and was commissioned on 30 August 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-322)[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-323) Five Cowries also serves as the headquarters of the Lagos State Waterways Authority (LASWA). It has a jetty, restaurant, bar, administrative offices, ticket offices, waiting area, toilets, lift, conference room, and ATMs. Boats from private individuals and other operators also dock at the pier. The terminal also has a multi-storey car park with space for over 800 vehicles. The multi-storey car park is located behind the terminal (as seen from the lagoon) and is directly connected to the terminal. The terminal was built without thresholds and there is a toilet for wheelchair users.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-1)[\[319\]](#refnote-:7) Five Cowries is open seven days a week, from 8.00 am to 5.00 pm. The terminal is located next to the Falomo Bridge. The destinations of LASWA ferries are Marina, [Ikoyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikoyi "Ikoyi"), [Victoria Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Island "Victoria Island"), Lekki, [Apapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apapa "Apapa"), [Ikorodu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikorodu "Ikorodu") and [Badagry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badagry "Badagry").[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-324)

## Healthcare

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=66 "Edit section: Healthcare")\]

Further information: [List of hospitals in Lagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Lagos "List of hospitals in Lagos")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Lagos-Nigeria-Duchess-hospital-IMG_6127cutoutimpro2b.jpg/250px-Lagos-Nigeria-Duchess-hospital-IMG_6127cutoutimpro2b.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lagos-Nigeria-Duchess-hospital-IMG_6127cutoutimpro2b.jpg) Duchess hospital in [Ikeja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikeja "Ikeja")

In the summer of 2014, Lagos’s often underestimated healthcare system proved its worth when it successfully contained an [Ebola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebola "Ebola") outbreak. Dr [Ameyo Stella Adadevoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ameyo_Adadevoh "Ameyo Adadevoh") was the specialist at the First Consultants Medical Centre in Lagos, Nigeria, who identified and isolated the first Ebola case (Patrick Sawyer) in July 2014. Her refusal to discharge the patient, despite diplomatic pressure, saved countless lives by containing the spread of the virus.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-325)[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-326)[&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-327)

### General and specialist hospitals

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=67 "Edit section: General and specialist hospitals")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/Lagos-Nigeria-Reddington-Hospital-Aug-2026-IMG_6412_cutout3improved-small.jpg/250px-Lagos-Nigeria-Reddington-Hospital-Aug-2026-IMG_6412_cutout3improved-small.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lagos-Nigeria-Reddington-Hospital-Aug-2026-IMG_6412_cutout3improved-small.jpg) Reddington Hospital on Victoria Island

Lagos has many hospitals and medical facilities. The [oldest Nigerian hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Island_General_Hospital "Lagos Island General Hospital") is located in the city as well as [West Africa's first air-operated emergency medical service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_Doctors_Nigeria "Flying Doctors Nigeria"), which commenced in the city. The Lagos healthcare system is divided into public and private sectors that provide medical services at the primary, secondary, and tertiary levels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-328)

Of the numerous hospitals in Lagos, the following are highlighted in particular in articles:

- Lagoon Hospital, private, international standard,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-:15) ‘one of Nigeria’s foremost private tertiary care providers’,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:20-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-:20)
- St Nicholas Hospital, private, international standard,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-1)[\[327\]](#refnote-:15) ‘multi-specialty tertiary care center with a legacy of clinical excellence’,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:20-1)[\[328\]](#refnote-:20)
- EKO Hospital, private, international standard,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-2)[\[327\]](#refnote-:15) ‘multi-specialty tertiary care center offering a broad spectrum of services’,
- Reddington Hospital, international standard,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-3)[\[327\]](#refnote-:15) ‘leading private hospital in Lagos, known for its comprehensive emergency services’, accredited by COHSASA,
- Euracare Multi-Specialist Hospital, ‘best urology clinic in Nigeria’,[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-331)
- [First Consultant Medical Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Consultant_Hospital "First Consultant Hospital"), private, ‘has treated Nigerian presidents’,
- Paelon Memorial Hospital, 5-star SafeCare award for quality.[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-332)

In 2025, at the “Prostate Clinic” on Victoria Island, West Africa’s first robotic operation using a minimally invasive procedure was successfully performed on a man with prostate cancer under the supervision of Prof. Kingsley Ekwueme.[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-333)[&#8202;](#ref-back-334-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-334)

## Quality of life

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=68 "Edit section: Quality of life")\]

Opinions on the quality of life in Lagos vary widely.

In 2023, Lagos ranked second – behind [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila "Manila") – in a global survey by the Statista Research Department of cities with the _lowest_ quality of life.[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-335) – By contrast, from 2024 through 2026, Lagos has featured each year in [Time Out magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Out_magazine "Time Out magazine")'s list of the "50 Best Cities in the World", based on the Time Out Index web survey of locals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-TimeOut)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-1)[\[335\]](#refnote-TimeOut)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-2)[\[336\]](#refnote-TimeOut) In the 2024 survey, 96 percent of respondents said they were happy,[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-3)[\[334\]](#refnote-TimeOut) and in every year, over two-thirds of respondents have said that it is easy to make friends in Lagos.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-4)[\[334\]](#refnote-TimeOut)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-5)[\[335\]](#refnote-TimeOut)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TimeOut-6)[\[336\]](#refnote-TimeOut) " [Business Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BusinessDay_(Nigeria) "BusinessDay (Nigeria)")" adds that Lagos remains a major cultural and economic centre characterised by its vibrancy, diversity and population density. The city has also made a name for itself through its music and creative industries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-339) In 2023, Brand Finance ranked Lagos fifth among the most desirable cities in Africa, behind [Cape Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Town "Cape Town") and [Johannesburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannesburg "Johannesburg"), [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo "Cairo") and [Casablanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casablanca "Casablanca"), but ahead of [Nairobi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nairobi "Nairobi"). The survey involved 15,000 respondents across 20 countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:17-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-:17)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:18-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-:18)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:19-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-:19)

By way of qualification, it must be made clear that index lists of countries or cities are usually not based on verifiable or locally collected data,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-:10) but often on subjective assessments by Europeans or North Americans. The [CPI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index "Corruption Perceptions Index") of [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International "Transparency International"), for example, is, according to its own definition, _"based on the perceived assessment of lay people and experts and is not reduced to actual experience and its analysis"_. There is criticism that the indices determined simply reflect the prejudices of Western countries[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-344) numerically.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-345)[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-346)

## Social situation, informal economy

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=69 "Edit section: Social situation, informal economy")\]

There is a huge spectrum of wealth distribution among the people that reside in Lagos. It ranges from the very wealthy to the very poor. Lagos has attracted many young people and families seeking a better life from all other parts of Nigeria and beyond.[&#8202;](#ref-back-first-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-first)[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-348)

In some parts of Lagos, residents have one of the highest standards of living in Nigeria and in Africa.[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-349)[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-350)

Reliable data on [unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment "Unemployment"), [income below the subsistence level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_threshold "Poverty threshold"), etc. are hardly available for Lagos – as for the whole region – and must be taken with skepticism where they are provided, e.g. in other articles (see also the previous chapter). The reason for this is the widespread " _[informal economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy "Informal economy")_" (not to be confused with " [shadow economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_market "Black market")") in West Africa. Insufficient jobs in traditional wage labour force people to look for work elsewhere. This benefits the informal sector of the economy, where there is no minimum wage and workers pay no taxes, have no holiday or labour rights and often work in unsafe conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-:11)

According to the [International Monetary Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund "International Monetary Fund"), about 5.5 million people are employed in the informal economy in Lagos State alone – about three-quarters of Lagos's workforce.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-:12) Throughout Lagos, one can observe street vendors, artisans, sellers, small and micro enterprises, shared taxis, tricycles and motorbikes (okada drivers), domestic workers, market traders[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-1)[\[349\]](#refnote-:11) and others engaged in the informal sector.

Activities in the informal economy are not included in economic statistics. As a result, the wealth of the population, but also e.g. unemployment, is significantly underestimated if the massive informal economy is not taken into account.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[350\]](#refnote-:12)

## Recycling, sewage and water supply

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=70 "Edit section: Recycling, sewage and water supply")\]

### Recycling/waste management

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=71 "Edit section: Recycling/waste management")\]

In Lagos, only 40 percent of waste is collected and only 13 percent is recycled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-:9) 13,000 tonnes of waste are generated daily in the metropolis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-354) Some residents burn their waste, which exacerbates pollution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-1)[\[351\]](#refnote-:9)

### Water supply

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=72 "Edit section: Water supply")\]

Tap water in Lagos is not suitable for drinking, but can be used for other purposes such as cooking and showering.[&#8202;](#ref-back-355-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-355) The water in the distribution network is often contaminated. Since the raw water in the lagoon is too polluted, the city draws its water from the Ogun and Owo rivers. There has been debate about the poor water quality in Lagos for years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-356)[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-357) At the same time, a sizable proportion of the residents live in slums without access to piped water and sanitation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-358)[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-359)[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-360)[&#8202;](#ref-back-361-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-361)

### Sewage

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lagos&action=edit&section=73 "Edit section: Sewage")\]

An efficient sewage system is lacking. Sewage is flushed into the open sewerage system by rainwater. This water then carries pollutants into rivers and the lagoon. Sewage also enters groundwater through leaking septic tanks and latrines. The contaminants can then contaminate the water in wells and boreholes. Water sold by street vendors can also be affected, as it comes from the same sources.[&#8202;](#ref-back-362-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-362)

[↑](#ref-back-361-0)In 1995, the [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank "World Bank") stated that "70 percent of Lagos' population lived in [squatted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squatting_in_Nigeria "Squatting in Nigeria") settlements in extremely poor environmental surroundings. Knee-deep floods sweeping raw sewage and refuse inside densely packed homes were frequent in neighbourhoods where overcrowding was the norm. While the average residential density for Lagos was about 260 people per hectare, the population density in slums was between 790 and 1240 people per hectare."
